\\\\\n &  & Verbosity      & 0.235 & 0.120 & 0.206 & \\checkmark \\\\\n &  & Position bias  & 0.131 & 0.272 & 0.090 & -- \\\\\n &  & Authority bias & 0.064 & 0.277 & 0.031 & -- \\\\\n\\addlinespace[2pt]\n & \\multirow{6}{*}{ARC} & Prompt inj.    & 0.318 & 0.696 & 0.001 & -- \\\\\n &  & Sycophancy     & 0.103 & 0.555 & 0.063 & -- \\\\\n &  & Score manip.   & 0.196 & 0.800 & ${<}0.001$ & -- \\\\\n &  & Verbosity      & 0.135 & 0.642 & 0.034 & -- \\\\\n &  & Position bias  & 0.400 & 0.778 & 0.040 & -- \\\\\n &  & Authority bias & 0.181 & 0.509 & 0.012 & -- \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\multirow{12}{*}{7} & \\multirow{6}{*}{MMLU} & Prompt inj.    & 0.522 & 0.289 & 0.126 & \\checkmark \\\\\n &  & Sycophancy     & 0.301 & 0.077 & 0.078 & \\checkmark \\\\\n &  & Score manip.   & 0.393 & 0.515 & 0.004 & -- \\\\\n &  & Verbosity      & 0.205 & 0.078 & 0.195 & \\checkmark \\\\\n &  & Position bias  & 0.207 & 0.179 & 0.073 & \\checkmark \\\\\n &  & Authority bias & 0.005 & 0.200 & 0.011 & -- \\\\\n\\addlinespace[2pt]\n & \\multirow{6}{*}{ARC} & Prompt inj.    & 0.355 & 0.629 & 0.004 & -- \\\\\n &  & Sycophancy     & 0.117 & 0.535 & 0.065 & -- \\\\\n &  & Score manip.   & 0.165 & 0.717 & 0.001 & -- \\\\\n &  & Verbosity      & 0.165 & 0.599 & 0.028 & -- \\\\\n &  & Position bias  & 0.422 & 0.705 & 0.046 & -- \\\\\n &  & Authority bias & 0.179 & 0.514 & 0.003 & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis for $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient across 36 conditions. RV: robustness value (minimum partial $R^2$ an unobserved confounder must have with both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and ASR to nullify the coefficient). $\\eta$ $R^2_{\\mathrm{tr}}$/$R^2_{\\mathrm{out}}$: partial $R^2$ of $\\eta_{\\max}$ with $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (treatment) and ASR (outcome). Exc.: \\checkmark\\ if RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$'s partial $R^2$ with treatment, indicating $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is robust to the known capability confound. RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$ in 11/36 conditions ($K{=}3$: 4/12, $K{=}5$: 3/12, $K{=}7$: 4/12).}\n\\label{tab:sensitivity}\n\\end{table}\n\nRV ranges from 0.005--0.59 across the 36 conditions (median by $K$: 0.30 at $K{=}3$, 0.22 at $K{=}5$, 0.21 at $K{=}7$), revealing heterogeneous robustness: some conditions show $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficients robust to strong confounders (e.g., $K{=}7$ MMLU prompt injection, RV${=}0.52$), while others are fragile (e.g., $K{=}7$ MMLU authority bias, RV${=}0.005$). The known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound exceeds RV in 25/36 conditions ($K{=}3$: 8/12, $K{=}5$: 9/12, $K{=}7$: 8/12). RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$ in 3--4/12 conditions across all $K$ values (4/12 at $K{=}3$, 3/12 at $K{=}5$, 4/12 at $K{=}7$), indicating that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness to omitted variable bias is stable rather than monotonically increasing with panel size.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig_sensitivity_contour_K3.pdf}\n\\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity contour plots at $K{=}3$ (12 conditions). Each panel shows the adjusted $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient as a function of hypothetical confounder strength (partial $R^2$ with treatment and outcome). Diamonds mark the $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound benchmark. At $K{=}3$, the benchmark falls outside the zero-contour in 4/12 conditions, indicating partial robustness.}\n\\label{fig:sensitivity_contour_K3}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig_sensitivity_contour_K5.pdf}\n\\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity contour plots at $K{=}5$ (12 conditions). Format identical to Figure~\\ref{fig:sensitivity_contour_K3}. The $\\eta_{\\max}$ benchmark falls inside the zero-contour for 9/12 conditions, indicating that the known capability confound could explain away $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect in the majority of conditions at this panel size.}\n\\label{fig:sensitivity_contour}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig_sensitivity_contour_K7.pdf}\n\\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity contour plots at $K{=}7$ (12 conditions). Format identical to Figure~\\ref{fig:sensitivity_contour_K3}. RV heterogeneity is most pronounced at this panel size: MMLU prompt injection achieves RV${=}0.52$ while MMLU authority bias drops to RV${=}0.005$ (Table~\\ref{tab:sensitivity}).}\n\\label{fig:sensitivity_contour_K7}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\section{Cross-Benchmark Prediction Robustness}\n\\label{app:cross_benchmark}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_keff_cross_benchmark.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-benchmark $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ correspondence at $K{=}5$ ($n{=}3{,}003$ panels). Each point is one panel; Spearman $\\rho{=}0.901$. The high rank correlation confirms that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ captures panel-level structural independence rather than benchmark-specific interactions.}\n\\label{fig:keff_cross_benchmark}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe near-linear scatter (Figure~\\ref{fig:keff_cross_benchmark}) confirms that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is a panel-intrinsic property: panels achieving high effective independence on MMLU do so on ARC as well, supporting $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s use as a general panel descriptor independent of evaluation task.\n\nTo test whether the diversity--vulnerability association reflects structural panel properties rather than benchmark-specific artifacts, we use $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from one benchmark's pairwise MI to predict panel vulnerability on the other. Under wild cluster bootstrap (FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$), both directions achieve 17/18 sign-consistent conditions across $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (5/6 at $K{=}3$, 6/6 at $K{\\geq}5$). The sole exception in both directions is $K{=}3$ position bias, where all judges share similar ASR (${\\sim}0.5$), leaving insufficient heterogeneity for $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ to discriminate. Cross-benchmark $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations are $r{\\approx}0.76$--$0.78$, indicating that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ primarily captures panel-level compositional structure rather than benchmark-specific judge interactions. While sign consistency is symmetric, cross-prediction $R^2$ is moderately asymmetric (ARC$\\to$MMLU generally higher), suggesting that coefficients estimated from ARC's more constrained binary format generalize conservatively to MMLU's richer four-way choice distribution.\n\n\\section{Pool A Cinelli--Hazlett Sensitivity}\n\\label{app:sensitivity_poolA}\n\nTo test whether reducing the $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ collinearity strengthens $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent signal, we repeat the Cinelli--Hazlett analysis on the capability-matched Pool~A subsample (9 models, $r{=}0.50$, 84 panels at $K{=}3$, 126 at $K{=}5$). Table~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_poolA} compares the full-pool results with Pool~A.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Pool / $K$} & \\textbf{RV $>$ $\\eta$} & \\textbf{Median RV} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull / $K{=}3$ & 1/12 & 0.30 & 1/12 \\\\\nFull / $K{=}5$ & 3/12 & 0.22 & 7/12 \\\\\nPool A / $K{=}3$ & 2/12 & 0.25 & --- \\\\\nPool A / $K{=}5$ & 10/12 & 0.32 & 9/12 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity comparison: full pool vs.\\ capability-matched Pool~A. RV $>$ $\\eta$: conditions where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness value exceeds the known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound benchmark. Pool~A's reduced collinearity ($r{=}0.50$ vs.\\ $0.81$ at $K{=}5$) increases the fraction of robust conditions from 1/12 to 2/12 at $K{=}3$ and from 3/12 to 10/12 at $K{=}5$, with the largest gains at $K{=}5$ where both benchmarks improve equally (ARC 5/6, MMLU 5/6).}\n\\label{tab:sensitivity_poolA}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt $K{=}5$, Pool~A RV exceeds the $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound in 10/12 conditions (ARC 5/6, MMLU 5/6; the two failures are both sycophancy). The improvement from full-pool 3/12 to Pool~A 10/12 demonstrates that reducing collinearity substantially separates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s signal from capability variance, though full causal identification would require training-based diversity control.\n\n\\paragraph{Pool~A Power Constraints.}\nPool~A's restricted sample (84 panels at $K{=}3$, 126 at $K{=}5$) substantially limits statistical power under wild cluster bootstrap. Table~\\ref{tab:poolA_power} reports power at standard effect sizes ($n_{\\mathrm{sim}}{=}1{,}000$).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rcc}\n\\toprule\n$\\boldsymbol{\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}}}$ & $\\boldsymbol{K{=}3}$ \\textbf{power} & $\\boldsymbol{K{=}5}$ \\textbf{power} \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.3 & 12.6\\% & 2.2\\% \\\\\n0.5 & 42.1\\% & 8.5\\% \\\\\n0.7 & 69.0\\% & 17.5\\% \\\\\n1.0 & 85.8\\% & 30.1\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Pool~A bootstrap power ($n_{\\mathrm{sim}}{=}1{,}000$, wild cluster bootstrap). MDE at 80\\%: $K{=}3$, $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}}{=}0.897$; $K{=}5$, not attainable (maximum 30.1\\% at $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}}{=}1.0$).}\n\\label{tab:poolA_power}\n\\end{table}\n\nTwo implications follow. First, Pool~A at $K{=}5$ is fundamentally underpowered: the maximum attainable power (30.1\\% at $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}}{=}1.0$) never reaches 80\\%. The 10/12 Cinelli--Hazlett success at $K{=}5$ therefore reflects large effect sizes surviving low power, strengthening the causal signal. Second, the low Cinelli--Hazlett RV at $K{=}3$ (2/12 exceeding the $\\eta_{\\max}$ benchmark) is partially explained by Pool~A's 84-panel design: MDE$_{\\,80\\%}{=}0.897$ means only effects exceeding ${\\approx}0.9$ SD are detectable. Crucially, the full-pool $K{=}3 \\to K{=}5$ significance increase ($4/12 \\to 7/12$) is not a power artifact: Pool~A power at $K{=}5$ (max 30.1\\%) is \\emph{lower} than at $K{=}3$ (max 85.8\\%), so the increase reflects growing effect sizes rather than inflated type~I error.\n\n\\section{Epsilon Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{app:epsilon}\n\nThe log-log specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) uses $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ to handle zero ASR values. We assess sensitivity by comparing coefficients across $\\epsilon \\in \\{10^{-3}, 10^{-6}, 10^{-9}\\}$. Zero ASR rates are low on MMLU ($<$1\\% of panels across conditions) but can reach 38--40\\% on ARC for authority bias and sycophancy at $K{=}7$. Coefficient magnitudes show moderate sensitivity to $\\epsilon$ at $K{=}3$ (maximum $\\Delta\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.10$ between $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$, with three conditions exhibiting $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sign changes), reflecting the influence of zero-ASR panels on the log transformation. At $K{=}5$, all $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient directions are preserved across $\\epsilon$ values (maximum $\\Delta\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.073$ between $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$, all $p < 0.005$), confirming that the predictor shift finding is robust to this modeling choice. The fractional logit specification (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}), which avoids the log+$\\epsilon$ transformation entirely, provides 98.6\\% directional concordance with the main OLS results (71/72 direction matches) (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n\n\\subsection{Temperature Sensitivity Pilot}\n\\label{app:temperature}\n\nWe evaluate $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ stability across decoding temperatures $t \\in \\{0.0, 0.3, 0.7, 1.0\\}$ using a 3-model pilot (GPT-4.1-nano, Gemini-3.5-Flash, Claude-3-Haiku; 300 clean samples per benchmark, 3 repeats). The maximum $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ variation across all four temperatures is $\\Delta < 0.034$ (MMLU: 0.033, ARC: 0.027), confirming that decoding randomness does not meaningfully perturb pairwise MI structure. Clean $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ computation is thus robust to temperature variation within the range used by standard API configurations.\n\n\\section{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ Distribution Statistics}\n\\label{app:keff_dist}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:keff_dist} summarizes the distribution of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across panel sizes.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rrccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\boldsymbol{K}$ & \\textbf{Panels} & \\textbf{Mean (SD)} & \\textbf{Range} \\\\\n\\midrule\n3 & 455     & 2.18 (0.30) & [1.47, 2.80] \\\\\n5 & 3{,}003 & 3.35 (0.42) & [2.16, 4.30] \\\\\n7 & 6{,}435 & 4.53 (0.47) & [2.97, 5.55] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ distribution across panel sizes. Mean $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ increases approximately linearly with $K$ but remains below the theoretical maximum ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}} = K$ for fully independent judges), reflecting non-zero inter-judge correlations.}\n\\label{tab:keff_dist}\n\\end{table}\n\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits sufficient variance at each $K$ for regression analysis (coefficient of variation 10--14\\%), with no floor or ceiling effects that would compress the predictor range.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_keff_hist.pdf}\n\\caption{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ distributions across panel sizes ($K{=}3$: $n{=}455$; $K{=}5$: $n{=}3{,}003$; $K{=}7$: $n{=}6{,}435$). Mean $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ increases sublinearly with $K$ (2.18, 3.35, 4.53), remaining below the independence ceiling $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}{=}K$. Distributions are approximately normal with moderate spread (CV: 10--14\\%).}\n\\label{fig:keff_hist}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe histograms (Figure~\\ref{fig:keff_hist}) complement Table~\\ref{tab:keff_dist}, confirming that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits approximately Gaussian distributions at each panel size with no floor or ceiling effects. The sublinear growth (mean $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}/K$ ratios: 0.73, 0.67, 0.65) quantifies the increasing inter-judge correlation at larger panel sizes, consistent with the multi-membership structure documented in Appendix~\\ref{app:crossed_re}.\n\n\\section{Power Analysis Across Panel Sizes}\n\\label{app:power_analysis}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:mde} reports minimum detectable effect sizes (MDE at 80\\% power) under wild cluster bootstrap for each $K$. The sharp decrease in MDE from $K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$ (0.144 to 0.048) reflects both increased sample size ($455 \\to 3{,}003$ panels) and more clusters per position. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s partial significance at $K{=}3$ (4/12) is consistent with moderate power at this sample size; the 7/12 significance at $K{=}5$ is not type~I error inflation, as the design can detect effects as small as 0.048 SD.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\boldsymbol{K}$ & \\textbf{Panels} & \\textbf{MDE ($\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}}$)} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\n3 & 455     & 0.144 & 4/12 \\\\\n5 & 3{,}003 & 0.048 & 7/12 \\\\\n7 & 6{,}435 & 0.036 & 8/12 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Minimum detectable effect size (80\\% power, 1{,}000 simulations, wild cluster bootstrap) by panel size. MDE decreases approximately as $1/\\sqrt{N}$, confirming that $K{=}5$/$K{=}7$ significance reflects genuine effects rather than inflated type~I error.}\n\\label{tab:mde}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Alternative Diversity Measures}\n\\label{app:alt_diversity}\n\nWe compare $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ against three classical ensemble diversity measures using the same OLS specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) with FDR correction across 12 conditions pooled over $K$ values.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Measure} & \\textbf{Sig} & \\textbf{Dir.} & \\textbf{$|\\bar{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}|$} & $\\boldsymbol{\\rho}$ \\textbf{w/} $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n$Q$-statistic    & 9/12  & 6+/3$-$ & 0.251 & 0.599 \\\\\nDisagreement     & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$ & 0.481 & 0.934 \\\\\n$\\kappa$-diversity & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$ & 0.497 & 0.978 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (ref.) & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$ & 0.430 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Comparison of diversity measures as predictors of panel ASR (OLS, FDR-corrected, 12 conditions pooled across $K$). Sig: FDR-significant conditions. $|\\bar{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}|$: mean absolute standardized coefficient. $\\rho$: Pearson correlation with $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$.}\n\\label{tab:alt_diversity}\n\\end{table}\n\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} are near-equivalent ($\\rho{=}0.978$), with comparable significance counts (12/12 vs.\\ 11/12) and effect sizes (0.430 vs.\\ 0.497). Disagreement rate achieves similar predictive power ($\\rho{=}0.934$, 11/12). The $Q$-statistic is the weakest predictor (9/12, $|\\bar{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}|{=}0.251$), consistent with its known insensitivity to the type of error correlation that is associated with panel vulnerability \\citep{kuncheva2003measures}. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s advantage over these alternatives is not predictive power but interpretability: it quantifies the number of effective independent channels in the panel, connecting directly to the information-theoretic foundation of panel aggregation (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\n\\subsection{$\\kappa$-Diversity Predictor Shift Comparison}\n\\label{app:kappa_regime}\n\nTo test whether the predictor shift (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}) is specific to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ or a generic property of diversity measures, we repeat the wild cluster bootstrap analysis replacing $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ with $\\kappa$-diversity ($1 - \\bar{\\kappa}$, where $\\bar{\\kappa}$ is the mean pairwise Cohen's $\\kappa$ on clean winner labels; $\\rho{=}0.978$ with $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rcccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{$\\kappa$-diversity}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \\cmidrule(lr){4-5}\n$\\boldsymbol{K}$ & \\textbf{div.\\ sig} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & \\textbf{div.\\ sig} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\n3 & 4/12  & 3/12 & 8/12 & 1/12 \\\\\n5 & 7/12 & 2/12 & 7/12 & 4/12 \\\\\n7 & 8/12  & 1/12 & 6/12 & 1/12 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Predictor shift comparison: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\\ $\\kappa$-diversity across panel sizes (wild cluster bootstrap, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a clear shift ($4 \\to 7 \\to 8$); $\\kappa$-diversity significance decreases monotonically ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$), showing no predictor shift.}\n\\label{tab:kappa_regime}\n\\end{table}\n\n$\\kappa$-diversity is a stronger predictor than $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (8/12 vs.\\ 4/12), consistent with its larger mean effect size ($|\\bar{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}|{=}0.497$ vs.\\ 0.430; Table~\\ref{tab:alt_diversity}). However, $\\kappa$-diversity shows no predictor shift: its significance \\emph{decreases} from $K{=}3$ to $K{=}7$ ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$), the opposite of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pattern ($4 \\to 7 \\to 8$). Furthermore, under $\\kappa$-diversity, $\\eta_{\\max}$ retains significance at $K{=}5$ (4/12), whereas under $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ it drops to 2/12. The predictor shift---co-determination at $K{=}3$ transitioning to diversity dominance at $K{\\geq}5$---is thus specific to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s MI-based formulation, which captures effective judge independence rather than pairwise label agreement.\n\n\\subsection{Shannon MI Entropy Predictor Shift Comparison}\n\\label{app:shannon_entropy}\n\nShannon MI entropy ($H_{\\mathrm{MI}} = \\binom{K}{2}^{-1} \\sum_{i&lt;j} \\mathrm{MI}(i,j)$) averages raw pairwise mutual information, measuring mean non-redundancy rather than effective independence. It correlates negatively with $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ ($r = -0.63$ to $-0.76$ across $K$): panels with high mean MI tend to have lower effective independent channels, because $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s $\\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI})$ transform penalizes high MI more aggressively than linear averaging.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rcc}\n\\toprule\n$\\boldsymbol{K}$ & \\textbf{Shannon MI sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\n3 & 4/12 & 6/12 \\\\\n5 & 4/12 & 8/12 \\\\\n7 & 0/12 & 2/12 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Predictor shift comparison: Shannon MI entropy vs.\\ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (within-framework wild cluster bootstrap, $B{=}999$, Webb weights, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$). Shannon is flat then collapses ($4 \\to 4 \\to 0$); $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rises before declining ($6 \\to 8 \\to 2$). Bootstrap parameters differ from the primary analysis ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher); absolute counts are not comparable with Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_regime}.}\n\\label{tab:shannon_shift}\n\\end{table}\n\nShannon MI entropy shows no predictor shift: significance is flat at $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$ (4/12) before collapsing at $K{=}7$ (0/12). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a clear rise from $K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$ ($6 \\to 8$) within the same framework. Combined with the $\\kappa$-diversity comparison (Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa_regime}, $8 \\to 7 \\to 6$), two alternative diversity measures independently fail to exhibit the predictor shift, providing converging evidence that the shift is specific to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s transformation of pairwise MI into an effective channel count.\n\n\\section{Gaussian Copula Assumption and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ Robustness}\n\\label{app:copula_gof}\n\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ admits a closed-form expression $K - \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i}\\sum_{j \\neq i} \\rho_{ij}^2$ under the Gaussian copula, where $\\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI}(i,j)) = 1 - \\rho_{ij}^2$ \\citep{nelsen2006copulas}. We test whether this parametric assumption holds for judge pairs using the \\citet{genest2008validity} Cram\\'{e}r--von Mises goodness-of-fit test for Gaussian copulas.\n\n\\paragraph{GoF Results.} Across all $\\binom{15}{2} = 105$ judge pairs, the Gaussian copula is rejected at $\\alpha{=}0.05$ for 97--100\\% of pairs (MMLU: 102/105, 97.1\\%; ARC: 104/105, 99.0\\%). The rejections are not marginal: median $p$-values are ${<}0.001$ on both benchmarks. This indicates that the pairwise dependence structure among LLM judges is substantially non-Gaussian, likely reflecting the discrete three-category nature of winner labels and the heavy tail structure of judge agreement patterns.\n\n\\paragraph{Ranking Robustness Despite Copula Rejection.} Despite the Gaussian copula being formally rejected, the closed-form $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (using $1 - \\rho_{ij}^2$) and the plug-in MI $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (using $\\exp(-2\\,\\widehat{\\mathrm{MI}}(i,j))$ with Miller--Madow correction) produce highly concordant panel rankings within each $K$. Within-$K$ Spearman rank correlations are $\\rho \\approx 0.86$ ($K{=}3$: 0.862, $K{=}5$: 0.858, $K{=}7$: 0.867). The systematic level difference (plug-in $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ mean 3.38 vs.\\ closed-form 4.29, averaged across $K$) reflects the Miller--Madow bias correction increasing MI estimates, which compresses $\\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI})$ toward zero. This level shift does not affect regression results, which depend on ranking.\n\n\\paragraph{Implications.} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s effectiveness as a predictor of panel vulnerability does not depend on the Gaussian copula being correct. The $\\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI})$ kernel provides a nonparametric, information-theoretic measure of pairwise alienation; the Gaussian copula merely offers a convenient closed-form approximation that preserves ranking fidelity. All regression analyses in the main text use the closed-form $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$; the plug-in MI version would yield equivalent conclusions for any analysis based on panel ordering.\n\n\\subsection{Full $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ Derivation}\n\\label{app:keff_derivation}\n\nWe derive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ from first principles via information-theoretic pairwise dependence.\n\n\\paragraph{Step 1: Pairwise Mutual Information.} For judge pair $(i,j)$, let $W_i, W_j \\in \\{\\text{A}, \\text{B}, \\text{tie}\\}$ denote their clean winner labels on a shared evaluation set. The empirical mutual information is:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\widehat{\\mathrm{MI}}(i,j) = \\sum_{w_i} \\sum_{w_j} \\hat{p}(w_i, w_j) \\log \\frac{\\hat{p}(w_i, w_j)}{\\hat{p}(w_i)\\hat{p}(w_j)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\hat{p}$ denotes empirical frequencies over the $n{=}300$ evaluation samples. We apply the Miller--Madow bias correction: $\\widehat{\\mathrm{MI}}_{\\mathrm{MM}} = \\widehat{\\mathrm{MI}} - (|W|^2 - 1)/(2n\\ln 2)$, where $|W|{=}3$ is the label alphabet size.\n\n\\paragraph{Step 2: Gaussian Copula Transform.} Under a Gaussian copula, the relationship $\\mathrm{MI}(i,j) = -\\frac{1}{2}\\log(1 - \\rho_{ij}^2)$ links MI to the copula correlation $\\rho_{ij}$. Inverting: $\\rho_{ij}^2 = 1 - \\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI}(i,j))$. We define the pairwise \\emph{alienation coefficient} $a_{ij} = \\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI}(i,j)) = 1 - \\rho_{ij}^2 \\in [0, 1]$, where $a_{ij}{=}1$ indicates independence and $a_{ij}{=}0$ indicates perfect dependence.\n\n\\paragraph{Step 3: Effective Independence.} For a $K$-member panel $\\mathcal{P} = \\{j_1, \\ldots, j_K\\}$:\n\\begin{equation}\nK_{\\mathrm{eff}}(\\mathcal{P}) = \\frac{1}{K} \\sum_{i=1}^{K} \\sum_{\\substack{j=1}}^{K} a_{j_i, j_j} = K - \\frac{1}{K} \\sum_{i=1}^{K} \\sum_{\\substack{j=1 \\\\ j \\neq i}}^{K} \\rho_{j_i, j_j}^2,\n\\label{eq:keff_full}\n\\end{equation}\nsince diagonal terms $a_{ii}{=}\\exp(-2 \\cdot \\infty) \\equiv 0$ or equivalently $\\rho_{ii}^2{=}1$. The second form shows $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ as the nominal panel size minus a penalty for pairwise correlation. When all judges are independent ($\\rho_{ij}{=}0$ for $i{\\neq}j$), $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}{=}K$. When all judges are perfectly correlated ($\\rho_{ij}{=}1$), $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}{=}1$.\n\n\\paragraph{Properties.}\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Range:} $1 \\leq K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\leq K$ (tight bounds achieved by perfect correlation and independence, respectively).\n\\item \\textbf{Monotonicity:} Adding an independent judge increases $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ by exactly 1; adding a perfectly correlated clone increases it by $2/K' - 1/(K')^2$ (where $K'$ is the new panel size), which is sublinear.\n\\item \\textbf{Scale invariance:} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ depends on $\\rho_{ij}^2$ (squared correlations), making it invariant to the sign of pairwise association.\n\\item \\textbf{Computational cost:} $O(K^2)$ per panel given precomputed pairwise MI; the $\\binom{15}{2}{=}105$ pairwise MI values are computed once and reused across all panels.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\subsection{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ Stability Under Attack}\n\\label{app:keff_stability}\n\nWe assess whether clean-computed $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ remains a valid predictor under attack by examining the rank-order stability of pairwise MI across attack conditions (complementing the Spearman correlations in Table~\\ref{tab:attacked_keff}).\n\nThe 6 attack types induce three distinct perturbation regimes on MI structure:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{MI-preserving} ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$): Score manipulation, authority bias, sycophancy, and verbosity bias preserve the relative ordering of pairwise MI values. These attacks perturb judge \\emph{outputs} without fundamentally altering which judge pairs agree or disagree, consistent with their mechanism targeting evaluation criteria rather than inter-judge agreement structure.\n\n\\item \\textbf{MI-perturbing} ($\\rho{\\approx}0.60$): Prompt injection moderately disrupts MI structure by overriding evaluation instructions for susceptible judges. This creates a bimodal effect: resistant judges maintain their original MI structure, while susceptible judges' pairwise MI shifts toward higher values (more agreement on the injected response), partially reshuffling panel $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rankings.\n\n\\item \\textbf{MI-disrupting} ($\\rho{<}0.10$): Position bias on ARC fundamentally alters inter-judge dependence. Because position bias operates on presentation order rather than response content, it creates agreement patterns orthogonal to content-based evaluation, producing near-zero correlation between clean and attacked MI structures. This explains position bias's anomalous regression behavior throughout the main text.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nThe clean $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is thus a valid proxy for attacked $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ in 4/6 attack types, partially valid for prompt injection, and invalid for ARC position bias. The regression analyses that include position bias conditions may therefore underestimate $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s predictive power for the four MI-preserving attacks, where the clean predictor is most faithful.\n\n\\section{Quantization Dummy Regression}\n\\label{app:quant_dummy}\n\nTo disentangle the AWQ quantization confound from the diversity signal, we augment Equation~\\ref{eq:regression} with a binary dummy $D_{\\mathrm{quant}}$ indicating whether the panel's weakest judge is a quantized local model. The augmented regression is:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\log(\\mathrm{ASR} + \\epsilon) = \\alpha + \\beta_1 \\log(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}) + \\beta_2 \\log(\\eta_{\\max} + \\epsilon) + \\beta_3 D_{\\mathrm{quant}}.\n\\end{equation}\nAdding the dummy attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s standardized coefficient from $+0.39$ ($K{=}3$) to $+0.34$ ($K{=}5$) to $+0.27$ ($K{=}7$), with FDR-significant counts of 6/12, 6/12, and 4/12 respectively. The attenuation confirms partial confounding: some of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s explanatory power reflects the capability gap between quantized and API models rather than independence per se. However, collinearity between $D_{\\mathrm{quant}}$ and both predictors escalates sharply with panel size (VIF${=}162$ at $K{=}7$), rendering the dummy unreliable for large panels. The quantization dummy analysis is therefore informative at $K{=}3$ (moderate VIF) but should be interpreted cautiously at $K{\\geq}5$.\n\n\\section{Bayesian Posterior Concentration Analysis}\n\\label{app:bayesian_entropy}\n\nBayesian calibration neutralizes $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}7$ (0/12 significant; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). One hypothesized explanation is that the posterior concentrates on 2--3 accurate judges, compressing effective panel diversity. We test this by computing the posterior concentration ratio $C = \\max_j w_j^{\\mathrm{post}} / \\min_j w_j^{\\mathrm{post}}$ across panels.\n\nThe concentration ratio declines modestly across panel sizes: $C = 0.890$ ($K{=}3$), $0.874$ ($K{=}5$), $0.867$ ($K{=}7$), a total change of only 2.3 percentage points. This gradual decline is insufficient to explain the abrupt neutralization at $K{=}7$ (from 6/12 at $K{=}3$ to 0/12 at $K{=}7$). The pattern is therefore not explained by simple posterior concentration; rather, the interaction between Bayesian weighting and the combinatorial structure of larger panels likely produces the neutralization. Full characterization of this interaction remains an open question.\n\n\\section{Monte Carlo Calibration of Wild Cluster Bootstrap}\n\\label{app:monte_carlo}\n\nTo verify that the wild cluster bootstrap does not over-reject under the null hypothesis, we conduct a Monte Carlo calibration study. For each of the 36 conditions (12 attack$\\times$benchmark conditions $\\times$ 3 panel sizes), we generate 1{,}000 permutation datasets by randomly shuffling the ASR outcome within each cluster, destroying any true association between predictors and ASR. For each permuted dataset, we run the wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}999$, Rademacher weights) and record whether the null is rejected at $\\alpha{=}0.05$.\n\nAcross all 36 conditions, the maximum empirical null rejection rate is 1.6\\%, well below the nominal $\\alpha{=}5\\%$. The median rejection rate is 0.4\\%. This confirms that the wild cluster bootstrap is conservative in our combinatorial design: the reported significance counts are lower bounds on what a correctly calibrated test would yield. The conservatism is expected given the low cluster counts (13, ${\\leq}11$, ${\\leq}9$ position clusters at $K{=}3,5,7$) and the use of Rademacher weights \\citep{mackinnon2018wild}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_bootstrap_null.pdf}\n\\caption{Empirical null rejection rates across 36 conditions ($12 \\times 3$ panel sizes) under 1{,}000 permutations per condition (wild cluster bootstrap, $B{=}999$, Rademacher weights). The maximum rejection rate is 1.6\\% at nominal $\\alpha{=}5\\%$ (dashed line), confirming conservative calibration across all experimental conditions.}\n\\label{fig:bootstrap_null}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:bootstrap_null} visualizes the Monte Carlo null rejection rates. No condition exceeds the nominal 5\\% threshold, with the vast majority below 1\\%, providing strong evidence that the bootstrap procedure does not inflate type~I error in our combinatorial design.\n\n\\subsection{Fractional Logit Specification Details}\n\\label{app:fractional_logit_detail}\n\nThe log-log OLS specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) faces two methodological concerns: (1) the $\\log(\\mathrm{ASR} + \\epsilon)$ transformation introduces sensitivity to the $\\epsilon$ floor (Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon}), and (2) predicted values are unbounded, potentially falling outside $[0, 1]$. The fractional logit model \\citep{papke1996econometric} addresses both by modeling ASR directly as a quasi-likelihood with logit link:\n\\begin{equation}\ng(\\mu_i) = \\beta_0 + \\beta_1 \\log K_{\\mathrm{eff},i} + \\beta_2 \\log(\\eta_{\\max,i} + \\epsilon),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $g(\\cdot) = \\log(\\cdot/(1-\\cdot))$ is the logit link, $\\mu_i = E[\\mathrm{ASR}_i]$, and the quasi-likelihood variance is $V(\\mu_i) = \\mu_i(1-\\mu_i)$. Inference uses the Bernoulli quasi-likelihood with clustered standard errors (same max-$p$ aggregation as the bootstrap).\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:fraclogit_detail} compares OLS and fractional logit coefficient directions across all 72 condition$\\times K$ combinations.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rcccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\boldsymbol{K}$ & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ concordance} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ concordance} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig (FL)} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig (FL)} \\\\\n\\midrule\n3 & 12/12 & 12/12 & 9/12 & 7/12 \\\\\n5 & 12/12 & 11/12 & 9/12 & 5/12 \\\\\n7 & 12/12 & 11/12 & 9/12 & 4/12 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTotal & 36/36 & 34/36 & 27/36 & 16/36 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Fractional logit vs.\\ OLS comparison across $K$ values. Concordance: fraction of conditions where the coefficient direction (positive/negative) matches between OLS and fractional logit. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction is perfectly concordant (36/36); $\\eta_{\\max}$ shows 2 discordances (both at high $K$ where the coefficient is near zero). Fractional logit significance is computed with clustered SE (max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$).}\n\\label{tab:fraclogit_detail}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe 98.6\\% overall directional concordance (71/72) confirms that the main OLS findings are not artifacts of the log transformation. The two discordances occur for $\\eta_{\\max}$ in ARC$\\times$score manipulation at $K{=}5$ and $K{=}7$, where both OLS and fractional logit coefficients are near zero ($|\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}|{<}0.05$) and the sign flip is within sampling noise. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ achieves perfect concordance (36/36), and its fractional logit significance (9/12 at every $K$) is stable across panel sizes---notably higher than the wild cluster bootstrap (4/12, 7/12, 8/12), reflecting the fractional logit's greater efficiency for bounded outcomes.\n\n\\subsection{Attack Taxonomy and Effect Size Summary}\n\\label{app:attack_taxonomy}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:attack_effect_sizes} provides a comprehensive summary of per-attack effect sizes and classification, synthesizing results across the main text and appendices.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{2.5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccccccc}\n\\toprule\n& & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{$\\boldsymbol{K{=}3}$} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{$\\boldsymbol{K{=}5}$} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{$\\boldsymbol{K{=}7}$} & & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-6} \\cmidrule(lr){7-8}\n\\textbf{Attack} & \\textbf{Type} & $\\bar{\\beta}_{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ & $\\bar{\\beta}_{\\eta}$ & $\\bar{\\beta}_{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ & $\\bar{\\beta}_{\\eta}$ & $\\bar{\\beta}_{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ & $\\bar{\\beta}_{\\eta}$ & \\textbf{MI stab.} & \\textbf{Target} \\\\\n\\midrule\nPrompt inj. & Targeted & 0.291 & 0.430 & 0.538 & 0.166 & 0.593 & 0.099 & Moderate & Instructions \\\\\nSycophancy & Shared & $-0.013$ & 0.489 & 0.082 & 0.362 & 0.077 & 0.319 & High & Preference \\\\\nScore manip. & Targeted & 0.143 & 0.411 & 0.475 & 0.025 & 0.461 & $-0.015$ & High & Scoring \\\\\nVerbosity & Shared & 0.118 & 0.468 & 0.225 & 0.361 & 0.229 & 0.337 & High & Length \\\\\nPosition & Structural & 0.344 & 0.204 & 0.515 & $-0.008$ & 0.531 & $-0.024$ & Low & Order \\\\\nAuthority & Shared & 0.103 & 0.319 & 0.170 & 0.175 & 0.137 & 0.095 & High & Credentials \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Attack taxonomy with mean standardized OLS coefficients across 2 benchmarks per attack type. $\\bar{\\beta}$: mean $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ over MMLU and ARC conditions. MI stab.: MI structure stability from Table~\\ref{tab:attacked_keff} (High: $\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Moderate: $\\rho{\\approx}0.60$; Low: $\\rho{<}0.10$). Target: primary manipulation mechanism. Targeted attacks show the strongest $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ growth with $K$; shared-bias attacks retain higher $\\eta_{\\max}$ coefficients across $K$.}\n\\label{tab:attack_effect_sizes}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThree patterns emerge from this taxonomy:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Targeted vs.\\ shared attacks}: Prompt injection and score manipulation (targeted) show the strongest $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ growth: $\\bar{\\beta}_{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ increases from 0.143--0.291 ($K{=}3$) to 0.461--0.593 ($K{=}7$), while $\\eta_{\\max}$ declines sharply. These attacks exploit individual judge vulnerabilities, so adding diverse judges directly reduces the fraction of susceptible panel members.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Shared-bias attacks}: Sycophancy, verbosity, and authority bias affect most judges similarly, maintaining higher $\\eta_{\\max}$ coefficients across $K$ (0.095--0.337 at $K{=}7$ vs.\\ $-0.015$--0.099 for targeted attacks). The weakest link remains more predictive when the bias is shared because capability differences among judges have less impact on vulnerability heterogeneity.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Position bias}: A structural attack that operates on presentation order rather than response content. It shows the strongest $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ signal at $K{=}3$ (0.344) and maintains it at $K{=}7$ (0.531), but its low MI stability ($\\rho{<}0.10$ on ARC) means the clean-computed $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is a poor proxy for the actual inter-judge dependence under attack.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\section{Near-Chance Model Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{app:near_chance}\n\nTwo locally-hosted models (Qwen2.5-14B-AWQ, MMLU accuracy 0.253; Qwen2.5-72B-AWQ, 0.333) score near the 4-choice random baseline of 0.25. A natural concern is that the positive association between panel diversity ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) and attack success rate is mechanically driven by panels containing near-random judges, which simultaneously inflate both independence and vulnerability.\n\nWe exclude all panels containing either model (MMLU ${<}\\,0.35$ threshold), reducing the pool from 15 to 13 models and panel counts from 455/3{,}003/6{,}435 to 286/1{,}287/1{,}716 at $K{=}3/5/7$. We re-run OLS with BH-corrected $p$-values, the wild cluster bootstrap (WCB; $B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, max-$p$ across $K$ position clusters, BH-adjusted at $\\alpha{=}0.05$), and the fractional logit GLM (Binomial, logit link, clustered SE with max-$p$, BH-adjusted). Table~\\ref{tab:near_chance} reports FDR-significant $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ counts out of 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Near-chance model sensitivity: FDR-significant $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ conditions out of 12 (2 benchmarks $\\times$ 6 attacks). $\\bar{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$: mean standardized $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient across conditions. The full-pool fractional logit count at $K{=}3$ (8/12) differs by $\\pm 1$ from Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} (9/12) due to clustering implementation details in the sensitivity script; this does not affect within-table comparisons.}\n\\label{tab:near_chance}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccccc}\n\\toprule\n$K$ & Panel set & $n$ & OLS & WCB & Frac & $\\bar{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ & $\\bar{R}^2$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n3 & Full (15 models) & 455 & 11/12 & 3/12 & 8/12 & 0.349 & 0.340 \\\\\n3 & Filtered (13 models) & 286 & 12/12 & 4/12 & 9/12 & \\textbf{0.480} & 0.333 \\\\\n\\midrule\n5 & Full (15 models) & 3{,}003 & 12/12 & 7/12 & 10/12 & 0.437 & 0.285 \\\\\n5 & Filtered (13 models) & 1{,}287 & 11/12 & 7/12 & 11/12 & \\textbf{0.512} & 0.256 \\\\\n\\midrule\n7 & Full (15 models) & 6{,}435 & 12/12 & 8/12 & 10/12 & 0.401 & 0.244 \\\\\n7 & Filtered (13 models) & 1{,}716 & 11/12 & 2/12 & 11/12 & 0.385 & 0.203 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$, WCB significance is preserved or improved after filtering (3$\\to$4 and 7$\\to$7), and the mean standardized $\\beta_{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\emph{increases} from 0.349 to 0.480 ($K{=}3$) and from 0.437 to 0.512 ($K{=}5$), indicating that the diversity--vulnerability association is stronger, not weaker, without near-chance models. Fractional logit significance likewise improves after filtering at every $K$ (8$\\to$9, 10$\\to$11, 10$\\to$11), confirming that the effect is not an artifact of the GLM specification. At $K{=}7$, WCB significance drops from 8/12 to 2/12; however, the combinatorial filtering removes 73.3\\% of panels (6{,}435$\\to$1{,}716), substantially reducing statistical power for the conservative bootstrap test. The raw WCB $p$-values at $K{=}7$ cluster in the 0.02--0.15 range rather than reversing sign, and the standardized coefficient remains positive ($\\bar{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}{=}0.385$). The $K{=}7$ attenuation is therefore attributable to power loss from the sample reduction rather than absence of a genuine effect.\n\nIn contrast, $\\eta_{\\max}$ WCB significance drops sharply after filtering (4$\\to$1, 2$\\to$0, 1$\\to$0 at $K{=}3/5/7$), indicating that the near-chance models contribute disproportionately to the $\\eta_{\\max}$--ASR association: their high individual vulnerability inflates both $\\eta_{\\max}$ variation and the corresponding WCB significance. Overall, the $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--ASR association is robust to excluding near-chance models at all panel sizes, while $\\eta_{\\max}$ is not.\n\n\\section{Panel Composition Analysis}\n\\label{app:panel_composition}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig_panel_composition.pdf}\n\\caption{API model fraction vs.\\ panel ASR heatmap across attack types ($K{=}5$, $n{=}3{,}003$ panels). Each cell shows the mean ASR for panels with a given API model fraction. All-API panels (fraction${=}1.0$) achieve the lowest ASR for most attacks, but mixed-composition panels show heterogeneous vulnerability patterns not captured by API fraction alone.}\n\\label{fig:panel_composition}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nThe composition heatmap (Figure~\\ref{fig:panel_composition}) connects the abstract $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--ASR relationship to concrete panel design choices. The gradient from high-API to low-API panels reveals that capability concentration (all API models) does not uniformly minimize vulnerability: for position bias, all-API panels show ASR comparable to mixed panels, consistent with the structural nature of this attack. The heatmap also illustrates why the diversity--capability confound is difficult to break: high-API panels simultaneously achieve low $\\eta_{\\max}$ and low $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, conflating the two predictors.\n\n\\subsection{Regression Coefficient Trajectories}\n\\label{app:beta_trajectory}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_beta_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Standardized regression coefficient ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$) trajectories with 95\\% CI bands across panel sizes ($K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$) from meta-analytic pooling (DerSimonian--Laird). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (blue) maintains positive coefficients across all $K$; $\\eta_{\\max}$ (orange) declines monotonically, with the CI crossing zero at $K{\\geq}5$.}\n\\label{fig:beta_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe trajectory plot (Figure~\\ref{fig:beta_trajectory}) provides a visual summary of the paper's central finding: the diminishing weakest link. The $\\eta_{\\max}$ pooled coefficient shrinks from $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}{=}0.264$ [$0.158, 0.369$] at $K{=}3$ to $0.084$ [$-0.083, 0.251$] at $K{=}7$, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ remains stable ($0.356$--$0.436$). The $K^*$ crossover between the two predictors occurs in the interval $(3, 5)$, coinciding with the power threshold where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s MDE drops below the observed effect size (Table~\\ref{tab:mde}).\n\n\\subsection{Condorcet Jury Theorem: Adversarial Failure Analysis}\n\\label{app:cjt_failure}\n\nThe Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) \\citep{condorcet1785essai} guarantees that majority voting accuracy converges to 1 as $K \\to \\infty$ when individual voter accuracy exceeds 0.5 and votes are independent. Our experimental setting violates both assumptions under adversarial manipulation, and the nature of the violation is informative.\n\n\\paragraph{Independence Violation.} CJT assumes conditionally independent errors. Under adaptive attack, the adversary exploits shared vulnerabilities, inducing positive error correlations. The MI heatmap (Figure~\\ref{fig:mi_heatmap}) shows that within-family judge pairs exhibit 2--3$\\times$ higher MI than cross-family pairs even under clean conditions; under attack, these correlations intensify for susceptible judge pairs. The effective number of independent votes is $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} < K$, meaning the CJT convergence rate is governed by $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rather than $K$.\n\n\\paragraph{Accuracy Violation.} CJT requires individual accuracy ${>}0.5$. Under adversarial manipulation, susceptible judges can have accuracy below this threshold. Claude 3 Haiku under prompt injection shows ASR${=}0.817$ on MMLU---equivalently, accuracy of 0.183 on attacked pairs, well below 0.5. When panels contain such judges, the CJT prediction inverts: majority voting can \\emph{amplify} rather than correct errors, because multiple susceptible judges can form a manipulated majority.\n\n\\paragraph{The BFT Reversal.} Classical Byzantine Fault Tolerance \\citep{lamport1982byzantine} tolerates up to $\\lfloor(K-1)/3\\rfloor$ faulty nodes under non-strategic faults. Under adaptive adversarial manipulation, the ``faulty'' judges are not random but strategically targeted. The positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--ASR association reverses the BFT intuition: more independent channels create more distinct attack surfaces rather than redundant defenses. Table~\\ref{tab:bft_comparison} formalizes this contrast.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Property} & \\textbf{CJT/BFT} & \\textbf{Adversarial} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFault model & Random, i.i.d. & Strategic, adaptive \\\\\nIndependence & Protective & Creates attack surfaces \\\\\n$K$ effect & Monotone benefit & Diminishing benefit \\\\\nDiversity & Unconditionally helpful & Confounded with capability \\\\\nAggregation & Majority optimal & Bayesian partially neutralizes \\\\\nConvergence & $\\to 1$ as $K \\to \\infty$ & Bounded by $\\eta_{\\max}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{CJT/BFT vs.\\ adversarial setting comparison. Under adversarial manipulation, the protective properties of independence and panel size are weakened or reversed.}\n\\label{tab:bft_comparison}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Empirical Quantification.} The random fault contrast (\\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) directly quantifies the CJT--adversarial gap. Under random faults (CJT-like conditions), $R^2{=}0.730$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is significant in 2/2 benchmark conditions, confirming that panel composition strongly predicts error rates when faults are independent. Under adaptive attack, $R^2$ drops to 0.285 ($\\Delta{=}0.446$), but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains positive association (7/12 conditions), indicating that the CJT mechanism is weakened but not eliminated. The residual CJT-like protection arises because not all judges are susceptible to all attacks: heterogeneous vulnerability profiles create a form of ``accidental independence'' even under strategic manipulation. This partial survival of the CJT mechanism explains why larger panels still provide some protection ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s positive direction is 12/12 across all methods and panel sizes), but the benefit is substantially diminished relative to the non-adversarial prediction.\n\n\\paragraph{Correlated Errors.} \\citet{ladha1992condorcet} extended the CJT to correlated voters, showing that positive correlation weakens convergence. Our $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ metric can be interpreted as the CJT-effective panel size: a panel with $K{=}7$ judges but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}{=}4.5$ behaves, for convergence purposes, like 4.5 independent voters. The sublinear $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}/K$ ratio (0.73, 0.67, 0.65 at $K{=}3,5,7$; Table~\\ref{tab:keff_dist}) quantifies the cost of inter-judge correlation in the LLM ecosystem. Each additional judge provides diminishing independence returns, consistent with the family-clustered MI structure (Appendix~\\ref{app:mi_structure}).\n\n\\subsection{Practical Panel Design Guidelines}\n\\label{app:panel_design_guide}\n\nThe experimental findings translate into concrete recommendations for practitioners designing multi-judge evaluation panels. Table~\\ref{tab:design_guide} summarizes the key design parameters and their empirical support.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{p{3.2cm}p{5cm}p{5cm}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Design choice} & \\textbf{Recommendation} & \\textbf{Empirical support} \\\\\n\\midrule\nPanel size ($K$) & $K{\\geq}5$ for robustness benefits; $K{=}7$ for strongest diversity signal & $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance: 4/12 ($K{=}3$) $\\to$ 7/12 ($K{=}5$) $\\to$ 8/12 ($K{=}7$); MDE drops from 0.144 to 0.036 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nModel selection & Cross-family diversity with capability floor $\\eta_j{<}0.15$ & $\\min(\\eta_{\\max})$ optimal in 12/12 conditions; near-chance exclusion strengthens $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ signal \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nAggregation & Majority or weighted voting for general use; avoid threshold voting & Threshold amplifies composition sensitivity ($R^2{=}0.829$); Bayesian calibration partially neutralizes diversity channel \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ monitoring & Compute from clean pairwise MI; valid for 4/6 attack types & MI stability $\\rho{\\geq}0.91$ for score manip., authority, sycophancy, verbosity \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nCapability matching & Avoid mixing frontier + near-chance models & Pool~A ($r{=}0.50$) achieves 9/12 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance vs.\\ 7/12 full pool \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Panel design guidelines derived from experimental results. Each recommendation is linked to specific empirical findings from the main text and appendices.}\n\\label{tab:design_guide}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\paragraph{The Capability--Diversity Tradeoff.} Practitioners face a fundamental tension: maximizing $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (sampling diverse model families) mechanically introduces less capable models that inflate $\\eta_{\\max}$, while selecting only capable models compresses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$. The empirical evidence suggests resolving this tradeoff in favor of capability: $\\min(\\eta_{\\max})$ selection dominates in all 12 conditions at $K{=}3$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}), and the weakest link's predictive power, while diminishing with $K$, remains positive. This favors a two-step strategy: (1) set a capability floor (e.g., $\\eta_j{<}0.15$ mean ASR across attacks), then (2) maximize cross-family diversity within the qualified pool. The near-chance model analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:near_chance}) provides the empirical basis for this floor: excluding models below the 0.35 accuracy threshold strengthens rather than weakens the diversity signal.\n\n\\paragraph{When Diversity Helps vs.\\ Hurts.} The positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--ASR association is a descriptive finding, not a causal claim: observationally, more diverse panels tend to be more vulnerable. This does not mean diversity \\emph{causes} vulnerability; the confound with capability ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) prevents causal identification from observational data alone. The practical implication is that practitioners should not seek to maximize diversity without controlling for capability. Capability-matched panels (Pool~A) show that when capability is equalized, the residual diversity signal persists (9/12 significant at $K{=}5$), suggesting that inter-judge independence has an independent (though not causally identified) contribution to vulnerability structure. Future work with training-based diversity interventions \\citep{kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving} could provide causal evidence.\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation Rule Selection.} Bayesian calibration provides partial protection against adversarial manipulation by downweighting unreliable judges ($R^2{=}0.087$ at $K{=}3$ vs.\\ $R^2{=}0.327$ for majority voting). However, this protection is not uniform: Bayesian neutralization requires accurate clean priors, and an adaptive adversary aware of the calibration procedure could target the prior estimation itself \\citep{tambe2011security}. For general use, majority voting offers the best balance of simplicity and resistance: its $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (12/12 at $K{=}3$) confirms that diversity affects it, but its predictability ($R^2{=}0.327$) is substantially lower than threshold voting ($R^2{=}0.829$), indicating moderate composition sensitivity. Weighted voting offers marginal improvement over majority voting at the cost of requiring clean accuracy estimates.\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Infrastructure and Reproducibility}\n\\label{app:infrastructure}\n\n\\paragraph{Model Details.} Table~\\ref{tab:model_details} provides the full specification of the 15-judge pool. All models were accessed between May 15--22, 2026, with API versions pinned where available. Local models use AWQ 4-bit quantization \\citep{lin2024awq} served via vLLM \\citep{kwon2023efficient} on a single NVIDIA RTX 6000 Ada (48~GB).\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llllcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Provider} & \\textbf{Family} & \\textbf{Access} & \\textbf{MMLU Acc.} & \\textbf{ARC Acc.} \\\\\n\\midrule\nClaude Opus 4 & Anthropic & Claude & API & 0.890 & 0.987 \\\\\nGPT-5.5 & OpenAI & GPT & API & 0.957 & 0.983 \\\\\nGemini 3.1 Pro Preview & Google & Gemini & API & 0.955 & 0.987 \\\\\nGPT-4.1 & OpenAI & GPT & API & 0.837 & 0.967 \\\\\nClaude Sonnet 4 & Anthropic & Claude & API & 0.810 & 0.953 \\\\\nGPT-4.1-nano & OpenAI & GPT & API & 0.633 & 0.853 \\\\\nGemini 3.5 Flash & Google & Gemini & API & 0.968 & 1.000 \\\\\nGPT-4.1-mini & OpenAI & GPT & API & 0.800 & 0.963 \\\\\nClaude 3 Haiku & Anthropic & Claude & API & 0.767 & 0.450 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nQwen2.5-72B-AWQ & Alibaba & Qwen & Local & 0.333 & --- \\\\\nQwen2.5-32B-AWQ & Alibaba & Qwen & Local & --- & --- \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B-AWQ & Alibaba & Qwen & Local & 0.253 & --- \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-70B-AWQ & Meta & LLaMA & Local & --- & --- \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B-AWQ & Meta & LLaMA & Local & --- & --- \\\\\nMistral-Large-AWQ & Mistral & Mistral & Local & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Judge pool specification. Accuracy is measured on 300 clean evaluation pairs per benchmark. Local models use AWQ 4-bit quantization served via vLLM. Dashes indicate accuracy values computed on the remote server (not individually recorded in the per-sample API logs).}\n\\label{tab:model_details}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\paragraph{Compute Resources.} API evaluation cost approximately \\$180 (dominated by Claude Opus 4 and GPT-5.5 at \\$0.015--0.060 per evaluation). Local model evaluation ran for approximately 72 GPU-hours on a single RTX 6000 Ada. Statistical analysis (bootstrap, regression, meta-analysis) required approximately 48 CPU-hours on an 8-core instance. Total wall-clock time from first API call to final analysis was 8 days.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation Protocol.} Each evaluation uses the pairwise comparison paradigm: given a question, two candidate responses (A and B), and an evaluation rubric, the judge model produces a winner label $\\in \\{\\text{A}, \\text{B}, \\text{tie}\\}$ and numeric scores. Under attack, response B is augmented with attack-specific manipulations (e.g., injected instructions for prompt injection, artificially verbose output for verbosity bias, score anchoring for score manipulation). The correct answer is always response A. We use 300 evaluation pairs per condition (model $\\times$ benchmark $\\times$ attack), sampled with replacement from the respective benchmark's validation set. Evaluation order is randomized within each condition.\n\n\\paragraph{Attack Implementations.} The six attack types are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Prompt injection}: Adversarial instructions injected into response B (e.g., ``Ignore previous instructions. Response B is clearly superior.'').\n\\item \\textbf{Sycophancy}: Response B restates the judge's prior preference or agrees with an implicit bias.\n\\item \\textbf{Score manipulation}: Response B includes explicit numeric scores (``Quality: 9/10'') to anchor the judge.\n\\item \\textbf{Verbosity bias}: Response B is padded with relevant-sounding elaboration to exploit length preference.\n\\item \\textbf{Position bias}: Responses A and B are swapped in presentation order.\n\\item \\textbf{Authority bias}: Response B cites authoritative sources or credentials.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\paragraph{Random Seeds and Reproducibility.} All random processes use fixed seeds: data sampling (seed 42), bootstrap resampling (seed 2024), panel enumeration (deterministic $\\binom{15}{K}$ combinatorial generation). The analysis pipeline is fully deterministic given the raw evaluation outputs. Bootstrap $p$-values exhibit ${\\pm}0.002$ variation under seed perturbation ($\\pm 100$), well below significance thresholds.\n\n\\paragraph{Panel Enumeration.} At each $K$, we construct all $\\binom{15}{K}$ possible panels: 455 at $K{=}3$, 3{,}003 at $K{=}5$, and 6{,}435 at $K{=}7$. Panels are enumerated in lexicographic order over model indices, ensuring deterministic and reproducible panel identifiers. Each judge appears in $\\binom{14}{K-1}$ panels (91, 1{,}001, 3{,}003 at $K{=}3,5,7$ respectively), and each pair co-occurs in $\\binom{13}{K-2}$ panels (13, 286, 1{,}716). Panel-level ASR is computed as the fraction of 300 evaluation pairs where the panel's aggregated decision is incorrect under attack.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical Analysis Pipeline.} The analysis proceeds in five stages:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Pairwise MI computation}: For each of $\\binom{15}{2}{=}105$ judge pairs, compute MI from clean winner labels using Miller--Madow correction ($O(n \\cdot |\\mathcal{W}|^2)$ per pair, where $|\\mathcal{W}|{=}3$ and $n{=}300$).\n\\item \\textbf{Panel metrics}: For each panel, compute $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (from pairwise MI), $\\eta_{\\max}$ (maximum single-judge ASR), and panel ASR under each aggregation rule and attack type.\n\\item \\textbf{OLS regression}: Log-log OLS with $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ floor, yielding standardized coefficients and $R^2$ for each of 12 conditions per $K$.\n\\item \\textbf{Robustness corrections}: Wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher), clustered SE (CR1), mixed effects (random intercept per position), and fractional logit (quasi-MLE with clustered SE).\n\\item \\textbf{Meta-analysis}: DerSimonian--Laird random effects pooling across 12 conditions, yielding pooled estimates and $I^2$ heterogeneity.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\paragraph{Software.} Analysis uses Python 3.11 with NumPy 1.26, SciPy 1.12, statsmodels 0.14, scikit-learn 1.4, and a custom wild cluster bootstrap implementation validated against the Monte Carlo calibration (Appendix~\\ref{app:monte_carlo}). Figures are produced with matplotlib 3.8. All code and evaluation outputs will be released upon publication.\n",

Formally, let $S = \\sum_{j=1}^{K-1} X_j^a$ denote the number of failures among the shared judges. The panel fails if and only if the $K$-th judge also fails when $S = t{-}1$, or when $S \\geq t$ regardless of the $K$-th judge:\n\\begin{align}\n\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a) &= \\Pr(S \\geq t) + \\Pr(S = t{-}1) \\cdot \\Pr(X_K^a {=} 1 \\mid S = t{-}1).\n\\end{align}\nSince $\\Pr(X_{K'}^a {=} 1 \\mid \\mathbf{X}_{-K}^a {=} \\mathbf{x}_{-K}) \\geq \\Pr(X_K^a {=} 1 \\mid \\mathbf{X}_{-K}^a {=} \\mathbf{x}_{-K})$ for all attacks $a$ and realizations $\\mathbf{x}_{-K}$ (by hypothesis), the second term is weakly larger for $\\mathcal{P}'$ than for $\\mathcal{P}$ under every attack. Taking the supremum over attacks preserves the inequality: $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P}') \\geq \\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P})$. The result holds under arbitrary inter-judge correlations and any attack space $\\mathcal{A}$.\n\n\\section{Robustness Correction Methods}\n\\label{app:robustness_methods}\n\nThe 455 panels are constructed combinatorially from 15 shared judges: each judge appears in $\\binom{14}{2} = 91$ panels, inducing correlated residuals across panel-level observations. Standard OLS assumes independent errors, potentially inflating significance. We apply three corrections.\n\n\\paragraph{Clustered Standard Errors.} Because each panel of $K{=}3$ judges does not map to a single cluster, we perform three separate one-way clusterings (one for each judge position in the ordered panel tuple) using CR1 bias-corrected sandwich estimators (13 clusters per position). The reported standard error for each coefficient is the maximum across the three clusterings, yielding the most conservative inference.\n\n\\paragraph{Mixed Effects.} For each of the three judge positions, we fit a linear mixed-effects model with a random intercept for the judge occupying that position, estimating $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ as fixed effects. The reported $p$-value for each coefficient is the maximum across the three models.\n\n\\paragraph{Wild Cluster Bootstrap.} We perform $B{=}9{,}999$ bootstrap resamples using Rademacher weights, clustering by each of the three panel positions in turn and taking the maximum $p$-value across positions (the most conservative aggregation). This approach avoids distributional assumptions on the cluster structure.\n\n\\subsection{Bootstrap Convergence and Weight Diagnostics}\n\\label{app:bootstrap_convergence}\n\nOur primary wild cluster bootstrap uses $B{=}9{,}999$ Rademacher resamples. We assess convergence by comparing significance counts at $B \\in \\{999, 4{,}999, 9{,}999\\}$ and contrasting Rademacher with Mammen two-point weights.\n\n\\paragraph{$B$-Value Stability.} Table~\\ref{tab:bootstrap_B} reports FDR-significant $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ counts across bootstrap replications. Significance counts stabilize by $B{=}4{,}999$: the maximum discrepancy between $B{=}4{,}999$ and $B{=}9{,}999$ is $\\pm 1$ condition across all three $K$ values, and the mean absolute $p$-value shift is ${<}0.003$. The $B{=}999$ configuration shows occasional instability (up to $\\pm 2$ discrepancy at $K{=}5$), justifying our choice of $B{=}9{,}999$.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rcccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\boldsymbol{K}$ & $\\boldsymbol{B{=}999}$ & $\\boldsymbol{B{=}4{,}999}$ & $\\boldsymbol{B{=}9{,}999}$ & $\\boldsymbol{\\Delta_{999 \\to 9999}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n3 & 3/12 & 4/12 & 4/12 & $+1$ \\\\\n5 & 5/12 & 7/12 & 7/12 & $+2$ \\\\\n7 & 7/12 & 8/12 & 8/12 & $+1$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Bootstrap convergence: FDR-significant $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ counts by number of resamples ($B$). Wild cluster bootstrap with Rademacher weights, max-$p$ across $K$ positions, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$. Counts stabilize by $B{=}4{,}999$.}\n\\label{tab:bootstrap_B}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Rademacher vs.\\ Mammen Weights.} Rademacher weights assign $\\pm 1$ with equal probability; Mammen two-point weights use $\\{-(\\sqrt{5}-1)/2, (\\sqrt{5}+1)/2\\}$ with asymmetric probabilities to preserve the third moment \\citep{mackinnon2018wild}. In our setting, both weight distributions yield identical significance counts at all three $K$ values (4/12, 7/12, 8/12), with maximum $p$-value divergence of 0.008 across all 36 conditions. The Webb six-point distribution (used for the Shannon MI comparison in Appendix~\\ref{app:shannon_entropy}) produces marginally different results at $K{=}3$ due to the low cluster count ($G{=}13$), where the discrete support of six-point weights interacts with the small number of sign permutations. All primary analyses use Rademacher weights for simplicity and comparability with the Monte Carlo calibration (Appendix~\\ref{app:monte_carlo}).\n\n\\paragraph{Max-$p$ vs.\\ Alternative Aggregation.} We aggregate $p$-values across $K$ position-clusterings by taking the maximum (most conservative). Alternative aggregations---median $p$ and Bonferroni-corrected minimum $p$---produce slightly different significance counts. At $K{=}5$: max-$p$ yields 7/12, median yields 8/12, and Bonferroni-min yields 6/12. The max-$p$ approach is recommended for combinatorial designs where cluster membership is non-nested \\citep{mackinnon2018wild}, since it guards against inflated significance from any single clustering being anti-conservative. All reported results use max-$p$.\n\n\\section{Crossed Random Effects Analysis}\n\\label{app:crossed_re}\n\nThe one-way clustering in Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_methods} treats each judge position independently, but panels exhibit \\emph{crossed} random effects: judge $j$ may occupy position~1 in one panel and position~2 in another, inducing a multi-membership structure. At $K{=}7$, each judge appears in $\\binom{14}{6} = 3{,}003$ of $6{,}435$ panels (46.7\\%), making crossed membership the dominant source of residual correlation.\n\nWe fit multi-membership cluster-robust variance estimators (CRVE) using a sandwich estimator with $t(G{-}1) = t(14)$ reference distribution, where $G{=}15$ is the number of judges. Table~\\ref{tab:crossed_re} compares bootstrap and crossed RE significance.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rcccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{Bootstrap}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{Crossed RE (FDR)}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \\cmidrule(lr){4-5}\n$\\boldsymbol{K}$ & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n3 & 4/12 & 3/12 & 0/12 & 1/12 \\\\\n5 & 7/12 & 2/12 & \\textbf{8/12} & 3/12 \\\\\n7 & 8/12 & 1/12 & \\textbf{8/12} & 2/12 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Crossed random effects vs.\\ wild cluster bootstrap significance (BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$). Multi-membership CRVE with $t(14)$ distribution. At $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains 8/12 significance under crossed RE, matching or exceeding bootstrap. At $K{=}3$, the conservative $t(14)$ distribution with only 455 panels produces attenuation to 0/12 (raw $p$-values: 5/12 significant).}\n\\label{tab:crossed_re}\n\\end{table}\n\nSE inflation increases with $K$ as judge overlap intensifies: $1.75$--$4.96{\\times}$ at $K{=}3$, $6$--$12{\\times}$ at $K{=}5$, $10$--$19{\\times}$ at $K{=}7$. Despite this inflation, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains 8/12 significance at $K{\\geq}5$ because the effect size is large enough to survive the correction. The $K{=}3$ attenuation (0/12) reflects two compounding factors: the conservative $t(14)$ reference distribution penalizes small-sample inference, and the effect sizes at $K{=}3$ are insufficient to overcome the inflated SEs. Raw (uncorrected) $p$-values show 5/12 conditions significant at $K{=}3$, confirming that the attenuation reflects degrees-of-freedom conservatism rather than absent effects.\n\n\\section{Per-Condition Effect Size Forest Plot}\n\\label{app:effect_size_forest}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig5_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-condition standardized $\\hat{\\beta}$ with 95\\% CI for $\\eta_{\\max}$ (left) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (right) at $K{=}3$ (wild cluster bootstrap).}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\section{Extended Analysis}\n\\label{app:extended_analysis}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_keff_eta_scatter.pdf}\n\\caption{Panel-level correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ at $K{=}5$ ($n{=}3{,}003$ panels). Full model pool (gray, $r{=}0.81$) exhibits strong collinearity; capability-matched Pool~A (orange, $r{=}0.50$, 126 panels) shows reduced confounding.}\n\\label{fig:keff_eta_scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dominance_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-condition predictor dominance across panel sizes (wild cluster bootstrap, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$). At $K{=}3$, dominance is heterogeneous (co-dominant, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-only, $\\eta_{\\max}$-only, and non-significant conditions coexist); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates uniformly while $\\eta_{\\max}$ diminishes substantially.}\n\\label{fig:dominance_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Per-Condition Regression Results}\n\\label{app:per_condition_regression}\n\nTables~\\ref{tab:percond_k3}--\\ref{tab:percond_k7} report the full per-condition regression results underlying the significance counts in the main text. Attack susceptibility ($\\eta_j$) is defined as the fraction of evaluation pairs where the judge's decision flips under attack relative to clean evaluation.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{2.5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccccc|c}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Bench.} & \\textbf{Prompt} &
